# Part IV

# Appendix

# A Deferred Proofs from Part II

# A.1 Proofs of Total Variation Properties

In this section, we present several deferred proofs from Sections 6.2 and 17. We first prove Lemma 6.3.

Proof of Lemma 6.3. This follows from a simple induction on  $m$ . Note that the case when  $m = 1$  follows by definition. Now observe that by the data-processing and triangle inequalities of total variation, we have that if  $\mathcal{B} = \mathcal{A}_{m-1} \circ \mathcal{A}_{m-2} \circ \dots \circ \mathcal{A}_1$  then

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {A} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {0}\right), \mathcal {P} _ {m}\right) \leq d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {A} _ {m} \circ \mathcal {B} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {0}\right), \mathcal {A} _ {m} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {m - 1}\right)\right) + d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {A} _ {m} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {m - 1}\right), \mathcal {P} _ {m}\right) \\ \leq d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {B} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {0}\right), \mathcal {P} _ {m - 1}\right) + \epsilon_ {m} \\ \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \epsilon_ {i} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows from the induction hypothesis applied with  $m - 1$  to  $\mathcal{B}$ . This completes the induction and proves the lemma.

We now prove Lemma 6.4 upper bounding the total variation distance between vectors of unplanted and planted samples from binomial distributions.

Proof of Lemma 6.4. Given some  $P \in [0,1]$ , we begin by computing  $\chi^2(\operatorname{Bern}(P) + \operatorname{Bin}(m - 1, Q), \operatorname{Bin}(m, Q))$ . For notational convenience, let  $\binom{a}{b} = 0$  if  $b > a$  or  $b < 0$ . It follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} 1 + \chi^ {2} (\operatorname {B e r n} (P) + \operatorname {B i n} (m - 1, Q), \operatorname {B i n} (m, Q)) \\ = \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {m} \frac {\left((1 - P) \cdot \binom {m - 1} {t} Q ^ {t} (1 - Q) ^ {m - 1 - t} + P \cdot \binom {m - 1} {t - 1} Q ^ {t - 1} (1 - Q) ^ {m - t}\right) ^ {2}}{\binom {m} {t} Q ^ {t} (1 - Q) ^ {m - t}} \\ = \sum_ {t = 0} ^ {m} {\binom {m} {t}} Q ^ {t} (1 - Q) ^ {m - t} \left(\frac {m - t}{m} \cdot \frac {1 - P}{1 - Q} + \frac {t}{m} \cdot \frac {P}{Q}\right) ^ {2} \\ = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(\frac {m - X}{m} \cdot \frac {1 - P}{1 - Q} + \frac {X}{m} \cdot \frac {P}{Q}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(1 + \frac {X - m Q}{m} \cdot \frac {P - Q}{Q (1 - Q)}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = 1 + \frac {2 (P - Q)}{m Q (1 - Q)} \cdot \mathbb {E} [ X - m Q ] + \frac {(P - Q) ^ {2}}{m ^ {2} Q ^ {2} (1 - Q) ^ {2}} \cdot \mathbb {E} \left[ (X - Q m) ^ {2} \right] \\ = 1 + \frac {(P - Q) ^ {2}}{m Q (1 - Q)} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $X \sim \operatorname{Bin}(m, Q)$  and the second last equality follows from  $\mathbb{E}[X] = Qm$  and  $\mathbb{E}[(X - Qm)^2] = \operatorname{Var}[X] = Q(1 - Q)m$ . The concavity of log implies that  $d_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathcal{P}, \mathcal{Q}) \leq \log \left(1 + \chi^2(\mathcal{P}, \mathcal{Q})\right) \leq \chi^2(\mathcal{P}, \mathcal{Q})$  for

any two distributions with  $\mathcal{P}$  absolutely continuous with respect to  $\mathcal{Q}$ . Pinsker's inequality and tensorization of  $d_{\mathrm{KL}}$  now imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {i = 1} ^ {k} (\operatorname {B e r n} (P _ {i}) + \operatorname {B i n} (m - 1, Q)), \operatorname {B i n} (m, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) ^ {2} \\ \leq d _ {\mathrm {K L}} \left(\otimes_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \left(\operatorname {B e r n} \left(P _ {i}\right) + \operatorname {B i n} (m - 1, Q)\right), \operatorname {B i n} (m, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} d _ {\mathrm {K L}} \left(\operatorname {B e r n} \left(P _ {i}\right) + \operatorname {B i n} (m - 1, Q), \operatorname {B i n} (m, Q)\right) \\ \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \chi^ {2} (\operatorname {B e r n} (P _ {i}) + \operatorname {B i n} (m - 1, Q), \operatorname {B i n} (m, Q)) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \frac {(P _ {i} - Q) ^ {2}}{m Q (1 - Q)} \\ \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof of the lemma.

![](images/8c0aa8526d29d624f16527a5afba4777f790b34c769eb4944d3cc1a600e1ef5f.jpg)

We now prove Lemma 6.5 on the total variation distance between two binomial distributions.

Proof of 6.5. By applying the data processing inequality for  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$  to the function taking the sum of the coordinates of a vector, we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\operatorname {B i n} (n, P), \operatorname {B i n} (n, Q)) ^ {2} \leq 2 \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\operatorname {B e r n} (P) ^ {\otimes n}, \operatorname {B e r n} (Q) ^ {\otimes n}\right) ^ {2} \\ \leq d _ {\mathrm {K L}} \left(\operatorname {B e r n} (P) ^ {\otimes n}, \operatorname {B e r n} (Q) ^ {\otimes n}\right) \\ = n \cdot d _ {\mathrm {K L}} (\operatorname {B e r n} (P), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)) \\ \leq n \cdot \chi^ {2} (\operatorname {B e r n} (P), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)) \\ = n \cdot \frac {(P - Q) ^ {2}}{Q (1 - Q)} \\ \end{array}
$$

The second inequality is an application of Pinsker's, the first equality is tensorization of  $d_{\mathrm{KL}}$  and the third inequality is the fact that  $\chi^2$  upper bounds  $d_{\mathrm{KL}}$  by the concavity of  $\log$ . This completes the proof of the lemma.

We conclude this section with a proof of Lemma 17.2, establishing the key property of reductions in total variation among recovery problems.

Proof of Lemma 17.2. As in the proof of Lemma 6.1 from [BBH18], this lemma follows from a simple application of the definition of  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$ . Suppose that there is such an  $\mathcal{E}'$ . Now consider the algorithm  $\mathcal{E}$  that proceeds as follows on an input  $X$  of  $\mathcal{P}(n, \tau)$ :

1. compute  $\mathcal{A}(X)$  and the output  $\hat{\theta}^{\prime}$  of  $\mathcal{E}^{\prime}$  on input  $\mathcal{A}(X)$ ; and  
2. output the result  $\hat{\theta} \gets \mathcal{B}(X, \hat{\theta}')$ .

Suppose that  $X \sim \mathcal{P}_D(\theta)$  for some  $\theta \in \Theta_{\mathcal{P}}$ . Consider a coupling of  $X$ , the randomness of  $\mathcal{A}$  and  $Y \sim \mathbb{E}_{\theta' \sim \mathcal{D}(\theta)} \mathcal{P}_D'(\theta')$  such that  $\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}(X) \neq Y] = o_n(1)$ . Since  $Y$  is distributed as a mixture of  $\mathcal{P}_D'(\theta')$ , conditioned on  $\theta'$ , it holds that  $\mathcal{E}'$  succeeds with probability

$$
\mathbb {P} \left[ \ell_ {\mathcal {P} ^ {\prime}} \left(\mathcal {E} ^ {\prime} (Y), \theta^ {\prime}\right) \leq \tau^ {\prime} \mid \theta^ {\prime} \right] \geq p
$$

Marginalizing this over  $\theta'$  yields that  $\mathbb{P}\left[\ell_{\mathcal{P}'}(\mathcal{E}'(Y), \theta') \leq \tau'\right.$  for some  $\theta' \in \operatorname{supp} \mathcal{D}(\theta)] \geq p$ . Now since  $\mathcal{A}(X) = Y$  is a probability  $1 - o_n(1)$  event, we have that the intersection of this and the event above occurs with probability  $p - o_n(1)$ . Therefore

$$
\mathbb {P} \left[ \ell_ {\mathcal {P} ^ {\prime}} \left(\theta^ {\prime}, \hat {\theta} ^ {\prime}\right) \leq \tau^ {\prime} \text {f o r s o m e} \theta^ {\prime} \in \operatorname {s u p p} \mathcal {D} (\theta) \right] \geq \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {A} (X) = Y \text {a n d} \mathcal {E} ^ {\prime} \text {s u c c e e d s} \right] \geq p - o _ {n} (1)
$$

Now note that the definition of  $\mathcal{B}$  implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ \ell_ {\mathcal {P}} (\theta , \hat {\theta}) \leq \tau \right] \geq \mathbb {P} \left[ \ell_ {\mathcal {P} ^ {\prime}} \left(\theta^ {\prime}, \hat {\theta} ^ {\prime}\right) \leq \tau^ {\prime} \text {f o r s o m e} \theta^ {\prime} \in \operatorname {s u p p} \mathcal {D} (\theta) \text {a n d} \mathcal {B} \text {s u c c e e s} \right] \\ \geq \mathbb {P} \left[ \ell_ {\mathcal {P} ^ {\prime}} \left(\theta^ {\prime}, \hat {\theta} ^ {\prime}\right) \leq \tau^ {\prime} \text {f o r s o m e} \theta^ {\prime} \in \operatorname {s u p p} \mathcal {D} (\theta) \right] - \mathbb {P} [ \mathcal {B} \text {f a i l s} ] \\ \geq p - o _ {n} (1) \\ \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof of the lemma.

# A.2 Proofs for To- $k$ -Partite-Submatrix

In this section, we prove Lemma 7.5, which establishes the approximate Markov transition properties of the reduction TO- $k$ -PARTITE-SUBMATRIX. We first establish analogue of Lemma 6.4 from [BBH19] in the  $k$ -partite case to analyze the planted diagonal entries in Step 2 of TO- $k$ -PARTITE-SUBMATRIX.

Lemma A.1 (Planting  $k$ -Partite Diagonals). Suppose that  $0 < Q < P \leq 1$  and  $n \geq \left( \frac{P}{Q} + 1 \right) N$  is such that both  $N$  and  $n$  are divisible by  $k$  and  $k \leq QN / 4$ . Suppose that for each  $t \in [k]$ ,

$$
z _ {1} ^ {t} \sim \operatorname {B e r n} (P), \quad z _ {2} ^ {t} \sim \operatorname {B i n} (N / k - 1, P) \quad \text {a n d} \quad z _ {3} ^ {t} \sim \operatorname {B i n} (n / k, Q)
$$

are independent. If  $z_4^t = \max \{z_3^t - z_1^t - z_2^t, 0\}$ , then it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} (z _ {1} ^ {t}, z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}), (\operatorname {B e r n} (P) \otimes \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q)) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right) + \sqrt {\frac {C _ {Q} k ^ {2}}{2 n}} \\ d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} (z _ {1} ^ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}), \operatorname {B i n} (n / k, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $C_Q = \max \left\{\frac{Q}{1 - Q},\frac{1 - Q}{Q}\right\} .$

Proof. Throughout this argument, let  $v$  denote a vector in  $\{0,1\}^k$ . Now define the event

$$
\mathcal {E} = \bigcap_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \left\{z _ {3} ^ {t} = z _ {1} ^ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t} \right\}
$$

Now observe that if  $z_3^t \geq Qn / k - QN / 2k + 1$  and  $z_2^t \leq P(N / k - 1) + QN / 2k$  then it follows that  $z_3^t \geq 1 + z_2^t \geq v_t + z_2^t$  for any  $v_t \in \{0,1\}$  since  $Qn \geq (P + Q)N$ . Now union bounding the probability that  $\mathcal{E}$  does not hold conditioned on  $z_1$  yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \mid z _ {1} = v \right] \leq \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathbb {P} \left[ z _ {3} ^ {t} <   v _ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} \right] \\ \leq \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathbb {P} \left[ z _ {3} ^ {t} <   \frac {Q n}{k} - \frac {Q N}{2 k} + 1 \right] + \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathbb {P} \left[ z _ {2} ^ {t} > P \left(\frac {N}{k} - 1\right) + \frac {Q N}{2 k} \right] \\ \leq k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {\left(Q N / 2 k - 1\right) ^ {2}}{3 Q n / k}\right) + k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {\left(Q N / 2 k\right) ^ {2}}{2 P (N / k - 1)}\right) \\ \leq 2 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third inequality follows from standard Chernoff bounds on the tails of the binomial distribution. Marginalizing this bound over  $v \sim \mathcal{L}(z_1) = \mathrm{Bern}(P)^{\otimes k}$ , we have that

$$
\mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \right] = \mathbb {E} _ {v \sim \mathcal {L} (z _ {1})} \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \mid z _ {1} = v \right] \leq 2 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right)
$$

Now consider the total variation error induced by conditioning each of the product measures  $\otimes_{t=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(z_1^t + z_2^t + z_4^t)$  and  $\otimes_{t=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(z_3^t)$  on the event  $\mathcal{E}$ . Note that under  $\mathcal{E}$ , by definition, we have that  $z_3^t = z_1^t + z_2^t + z_4^t$  for each  $t \in [k]$ . By the conditioning property of  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$  in Fact 6.2, we have

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {1} ^ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}\right), \mathcal {L} \left(\left(z _ {3} ^ {t}: t \in [ k ]\right) | \mathcal {E}\right)\right) \leq \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \right]
$$

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {3} ^ {t}\right), \mathcal {L} \left(\left(z _ {3} ^ {t}: t \in [ k ]\right) \mid \mathcal {E}\right)\right) \leq \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \right]
$$

The fact that  $\otimes_{t=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(z_3^t) = \operatorname{Bin}(n/k, Q)^{\otimes k}$  and the triangle inequality now imply that

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {1} ^ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}\right), \operatorname {B i n} \left(n / k, Q\right) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \leq 2 \cdot \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \right] \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right)
$$

which proves the second inequality in the statement of the lemma. It suffices to establish the first inequality. A similar conditioning step as above shows that for all  $v \in \{0,1\}^k$ , we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(v _ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t} \mid z _ {1} ^ {t} = v _ {t}\right), \mathcal {L} \left(\left(v _ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}: t \in [ k ]\right) \mid z _ {1} = v \text {a n d} \mathcal {E}\right)\right) \leq \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \mid z _ {1} = v \right] \\ d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {3} ^ {t} \mid z _ {1} ^ {t} = v _ {t}\right), \mathcal {L} \left(\left(z _ {3} ^ {t}: t \in [ k ]\right) \mid z _ {1} = v \text {a n d} \mathcal {E}\right)\right) \leq \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathcal {E} ^ {C} \mid z _ {1} = v \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

The triangle inequality and the fact that  $z_{3} \sim \mathrm{Bin}(n / k, Q)^{\otimes k}$  is independent of  $z_{1}$  implies that

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(v _ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t} \mid z _ {1} ^ {t} = v _ {t}\right), \operatorname {B i n} (n / k, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right)
$$

By Lemma 6.4 applied with  $P_{t} = v_{t}\in \{0,1\}$ , we also have that

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} (v _ {t} + \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q)), \operatorname {B i n} (n / k, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \leq \sqrt {\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \frac {k (v _ {t} - Q) ^ {2}}{2 n Q (1 - Q)}} \leq \sqrt {\frac {C _ {Q} k ^ {2}}{2 n}}
$$

The triangle now implies that for each  $v\in \{0,1\} ^k$

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t} \mid z _ {1} ^ {t} = v _ {t}\right), \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \\ = d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(v _ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t} \mid z _ {1} ^ {t} = v _ {t}\right), \otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} (v _ {t} + \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q))\right) \\ \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right) + \sqrt {\frac {C _ {Q} k ^ {2}}{2 n}} \\ \end{array}
$$

We now marginalize over  $v \sim \mathcal{L}(z_1) = \mathrm{Bern}(P)^{\otimes k}$ . The conditioning on a random variable property of  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$  in Fact 6.2 implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {1} ^ {t}, z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}\right), (\operatorname {B e r n} (P) \otimes \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q)) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {E} _ {v \sim \operatorname {B e r n} (P) ^ {\otimes k}} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t} \mid z _ {1} ^ {t} = v _ {t}\right), \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

which, when combined with the inequalities above, completes the proof of the lemma.

We now apply this lemma to prove Lemma 7.5. The proof of this lemma is a  $k$ -partite variant of the argument used to prove Theorem 6.1 in [BBH19]. However, it involves several technical subtleties that do not arise in the non  $k$ -partite case.

Proof of Lemma 7.5. Fix some subset  $R \subseteq [N]$  such that  $|R \cap E_i| = 1$  for each  $i \in [k]$ . We will first show that  $\mathcal{A}$  maps an input  $G \sim \mathcal{G}(N, R, p, q)$  approximately in total variation to a sample from the planted submatrix distribution  $\mathcal{M}_{[n] \times [n]}(\mathcal{U}_n(F), \operatorname{Bern}(p), \operatorname{Bern}(Q))$ . By AM-GM, we have that

$$
\sqrt {p q} \leq \frac {p + q}{2} = 1 - \frac {(1 - p) + (1 - q)}{2} \leq 1 - \sqrt {(1 - p) (1 - q)}
$$

If  $p \neq 1$ , it follows that  $P = p > Q = 1 - \sqrt{(1 - p)(1 - q)}$ . This implies that  $\frac{1 - p}{1 - q} = \left(\frac{1 - P}{1 - Q}\right)^2$  and the inequality above rearranges to  $\left(\frac{P}{Q}\right)^2 \leq \frac{p}{q}$ . If  $p = 1$ , then  $Q = \sqrt{q}$  and  $\left(\frac{P}{Q}\right)^2 = \frac{p}{q}$ . Furthermore, the inequality  $\frac{1 - p}{1 - q} \leq \left(\frac{1 - P}{1 - Q}\right)^2$  holds trivially. Therefore we may apply Lemma 7.3, which implies that  $(G_1, G_2) \sim \mathcal{G}(N, R, p, Q)^{\otimes 2}$ .

Let the random set  $U = \{\pi_1^{-1}(R\cap E_1),\pi_2^{-1}(R\cap E_2),\ldots ,\pi_k^{-1}(R\cap E_k)\}$  denote the support of the  $k$  -subset of  $[n]$  that  $R$  is mapped to in the embedding step of TO-  $k$  PARTITE-SUBMATRIX. Now fix some  $k$  -subset  $R^{\prime}\subseteq [n]$  with  $|R^{\prime}\cap F_{i}| = 1$  for each  $i\in [k]$  and consider the distribution of  $M_{\mathrm{PD}}$  conditioned on the event  $U = R^{\prime}$ . Since  $(G_{1},G_{2})\sim \mathcal{G}(n,R,p,Q)^{\otimes 2}$ , Step 2 of TO-  $k$  PARTITE-SUBMATRIX ensures that the off-diagonal entries of  $M_{\mathrm{PD}}$ , given this conditioning, are independent and distributed as follows:

-  $M_{ij} \sim \operatorname{Bern}(p)$  if  $i \neq j$  and  $i, j \in R'$ ; and  
-  $M_{ij} \sim \operatorname{Bern}(Q)$  if  $i \neq j$  and  $i \notin R'$  or  $j \notin R'$ .

which match the corresponding entries of  $\mathcal{M}_{[n]\times [n]}(R'\times R',\operatorname {Bern}(p),\operatorname {Bern}(Q))$  . Furthermore, these entries are independent of the vector  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}}) = ((M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{ii}:i\in [k])$  of the diagonal entries of  $M_{\mathrm{PD}}$  . It therefore follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} \left(M _ {\mathrm {P D}} \mid U = R ^ {\prime}\right), \mathcal {M} _ {[ n ] \times [ n ]} \left(R ^ {\prime} \times R ^ {\prime}, \operatorname {B e r n} (p), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)\right)\right) \\ = d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} \left(\operatorname {d i a g} \left(M _ {\mathrm {P D}}\right) \mid U = R ^ {\prime}\right), \mathcal {M} _ {[ n ]} \left(R ^ {\prime}, \operatorname {B e r n} (p), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Let  $(S_1', S_2', \ldots, S_k')$  be any tuple of fixed subsets such that  $|S_t'| = N / k$ ,  $S_i' \subseteq F_t$  and  $R' \cap F_t \in S_t'$  for each  $t \in [k]$ . Now consider the distribution of  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})$  conditioned on both  $U = R'$  and  $(S_1, S_2, \ldots, S_k) = (S_1', S_2', \ldots, S_k')$ . It holds by construction that the  $k$  vectors  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{F_t}$  are independent for  $t \in [k]$  and each distributed as follows:

-  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{S_t'}$  is an exchangeable distribution on  $\{0,1\}^{N / k}$  with support of size  $s_1^t \sim \operatorname{Bin}(N / k,p)$ , by construction. This implies that  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{S_t'} \sim \operatorname{Bern}(p)^{\otimes N / k}$ . This can trivially be restated as  $\left(M_{R'\cap F_t,R'\cap F_t},\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{S_t'\backslash R'}\right) \sim \operatorname{Bern}(p) \otimes \operatorname{Bern}(p)^{\otimes N / k - 1}$ .  
-  $\operatorname{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{F_t \setminus S_t'}$  is an exchangeable distribution on  $\{0,1\}^{N / k}$  with support of size  $z_4^t = \max \{s_2^t - s_1^t, 0\}$ . Furthermore,  $\operatorname{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{F_t \setminus S_t'}$  is independent of  $\operatorname{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{S_t'}$ .

For each  $t \in [k]$ , let  $z_1^t = M_{R' \cap F_t, R' \cap F_t} \sim \operatorname{Bern}(p)$  and  $z_2^t \sim \operatorname{Bin}(N / k - 1, p)$  be the size of the support of  $\operatorname{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{S' \setminus R'}$ . As shown discussed in the first point above, we have that  $z_1^t$  and  $z_2^t$  are independent and  $z_1^t + z_2^t = s_1^t$ .

Now consider the distribution of  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})$  relaxed to only be conditioned on  $U = R^{\prime}$ , and no longer on  $(S_1, S_2, \ldots, S_k) = (S_1', S_2', \ldots, S_k')$ . Conditioned on  $U = R^{\prime}$ , the  $S_t$  are independent and each uniformly distributed among all  $N / k$  size subsets of  $F_t$  that contain the element  $R' \cap F_t$ . In particular, this implies that the distribution of  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{F_t \setminus R'}$  is an exchangeable distribution on  $\{0, 1\}^{n / k - 1}$  with support size  $z_2^t + z_4^t$  for each  $t$ . Note that any  $v \sim \mathcal{M}_{[n]}(R', \operatorname{Bern}(p), \operatorname{Bern}(Q))$  also satisfies that  $v_{F_t \setminus R'}$  is exchangeable. This implies that  $\mathcal{M}_{[n]}(R', \operatorname{Bern}(p), \operatorname{Bern}(Q))$  and  $\mathrm{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})$  are identically distributed when conditioned on their entries with indices in  $R'$  and on their support sizes within the  $k$  sets of indices  $F_t \setminus R'$ . The conditioning property of Fact 6.2 therefore implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} \left(\operatorname {d i a g} \left(M _ {\mathrm {P D}}\right) \mid U = R ^ {\prime}\right), \mathcal {M} _ {[ n ]} \left(R ^ {\prime}, \operatorname {B e r n} (p), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)\right)\right) \\ \leq d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {1} ^ {t}, z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}\right), (\operatorname {B e r n} (p) \otimes \operatorname {B i n} (n / k - 1, Q)) ^ {\otimes k}\right) \\ \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right) + \sqrt {\frac {C _ {Q} k ^ {2}}{2 n}} \\ \end{array}
$$

by the first inequality in Lemma A.1. Now observe that  $U \sim \mathcal{U}_n(F)$  and thus marginalizing over  $R' \sim \mathcal{L}(U) = \mathcal{U}_n(F)$  and applying the conditioning property of Fact 6.2 yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {A} (G (N, R, p, q)), \mathcal {M} _ {[ n ] \times [ n ]} \left(\mathcal {U} _ {n} (F), \operatorname {B e r n} (p), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)\right)\right) \\ \leq \mathbb {E} _ {R ^ {\prime} \sim \mathcal {U} _ {n} (F)} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} \left(M _ {\mathrm {P D}} \mid U = R ^ {\prime}\right), \mathcal {M} _ {[ n ] \times [ n ]} \left(R ^ {\prime} \times R ^ {\prime}, \operatorname {B e r n} (p), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q)\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $M_{\mathrm{PD}} \sim \mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}(N,R,p,q))$ . Applying an identical marginalization over  $R \sim \mathcal{U}_N(E)$  completes the proof of the first inequality in the lemma statement.

It suffices to consider the case where  $G \sim \mathcal{G}(N, q)$ , which follows from an analogous but simpler argument. By Lemma 7.3, we have that  $(G_1, G_2) \sim \mathcal{G}(N, Q)^{\otimes 2}$ . It follows that the entries of  $M_{\mathrm{PD}}$  are distributed as  $(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{ij} \sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}} \operatorname{Bern}(Q)$  for all  $i \neq j$  independently of  $\operatorname{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})$ . Now note that the  $k$  vectors  $\operatorname{diag}(M_{\mathrm{PD}})_{F_t}$  for  $t \in [k]$  are each exchangeable and have support size  $s_1^t + \max \{s_2^t - s_1^t, 0\} = z_1^t + z_2^t + z_4^t$  where  $z_1^t \sim \operatorname{Bern}(p)$ ,  $z_2^t \sim \operatorname{Bin}(N/k-1,p)$  and  $s_2^t \sim \operatorname{Bin}(n/k,Q)$  are independent. By the same argument as above, we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} \left(M _ {\mathrm {P D}}\right), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q) ^ {\otimes n \times n}\right) = d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} (\operatorname {d i a g} \left(M _ {\mathrm {P D}}\right)), \operatorname {B e r n} (Q) ^ {\otimes n}\right) \\ = d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\otimes_ {t = 1} ^ {k} \mathcal {L} \left(z _ {1} ^ {t} + z _ {2} ^ {t} + z _ {4} ^ {t}\right), \operatorname {B i n} (n / k, Q)\right) \\ \leq 4 k \cdot \exp \left(- \frac {Q ^ {2} N ^ {2}}{4 8 P k n}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

by Lemma A.1. Since  $M_{\mathrm{PD}} \sim \mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}(N, q))$ , this completes the proof of the lemma.

# A.3 Proofs for Symmetric 3-ary Rejection Kernels

In this section, we establish the approximate Markov transition properties for symmetric 3-ary rejection kernels introduced in Section 7.3.

Proof of Lemma 7.7. Define  $\mathcal{L}_1, \mathcal{L}_2: X \to \mathbb{R}$  to be

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {1} (x) = \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {+}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {-}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) = \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {+}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) + \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {-}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - 2
$$

Note that if  $x \in S$ , then the triangle inequality implies that

$$
P _ {A} (x, 1) \leq \frac {1}{2} \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 | \mu_ {2} |} \cdot | \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) | + \frac {1}{4 | \mu_ {1} |} \cdot | \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x) |\right) \leq 1
$$

$$
P _ {A} (x, 1) \geq \frac {1}{2} \left(1 - \frac {a}{4 | \mu_ {2} |} \cdot | \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) | - \frac {1}{4 | \mu_ {1} |} \cdot | \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x) |\right) \geq 0
$$

Similar computations show that  $0 \leq P_A(x, 0) \leq 1$  and  $0 \leq P_A(x, -1) \leq 1$ , implying that each of these probabilities is well-defined. Now let  $R_1 = \mathbb{P}_{X \sim \mathcal{P}_+}[X \in S]$ ,  $R_0 = \mathbb{P}_{X \sim \mathcal{Q}}[X \in S]$  and  $R_{-1} = \mathbb{P}_{X \sim \mathcal{P}_-}[X \in S]$  where  $R_1, R_0, R_{-1} \geq 1 - \delta$  by assumption.

We now define several useful events. For the sake of analysis, consider continuing to iterate Step 2 even after  $z$  is set for the first time for a total of  $N$  iterations. Let  $A_{i}^{1}$ ,  $A_{i}^{0}$  and  $A_{i}^{-1}$  be the events that  $z$  is set in the  $i$ th iteration of Step 2 when  $B = 1$ ,  $B = 0$  and  $B = -1$ , respectively. Let  $B_{i}^{1} = (A_{1}^{1})^{C} \cap (A_{2}^{1})^{C} \cap \dots \cap (A_{i-1}^{1})^{C} \cap A_{i}^{1}$  be the event that  $z$  is set for the first time in the  $i$ th iteration of Step 2. Let  $C^{1} = A_{1}^{1} \cup A_{2}^{1} \cup \dots \cup A_{N}^{1}$  be the event that  $z$  is set in some iteration of Step 2. Define  $B_{i}^{0}, C^{0}, B_{i}^{-1}$  and  $C^{-1}$  analogously. Let  $z_{0}$  be the initialization of  $z$  in Step 1.

Now let  $Z_{1} \sim \mathcal{D}_{1} = \mathcal{L}(3\text{-SRK}(1))$ ,  $Z_{0} \sim \mathcal{D}_{0} = \mathcal{L}(3\text{-SRK}(0))$  and  $Z_{-1} \sim \mathcal{D}_{-1} = \mathcal{L}(3\text{-SRK}(-1))$ . Note that  $\mathcal{L}(Z_{t}|B_{i}^{t}) = \mathcal{L}(Z_{t}|A_{i}^{t})$  for each  $t \in \{-1,0,1\}$  since  $A_{i}^{t}$  is independent of  $A_{1}^{t}, A_{2}^{t}, \ldots, A_{i - 1}^{t}$  and the sample  $z'$  chosen in the  $i$ th iteration of Step 2. The independence between Steps 2.1 and 2.3 implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ A _ {i} ^ {1} \right] = \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \frac {1}{2} \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x) \right] \\ = \frac {1}{2} R _ {0} + \frac {a}{8 \mu_ {2}} \left(R _ {1} + R _ {- 1} - 2 R _ {0}\right) + \frac {1}{8 \mu_ {1}} \left(R _ {1} - R _ {- 1}\right) \geq \frac {1}{2} - \frac {\delta}{2} \left(1 + \frac {a}{2} | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1} + \frac {1}{4} | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ A _ {i} ^ {0} \right] = \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \frac {1}{2} \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x) \right] \\ = \frac {1}{2} R _ {0} - \frac {1 - a}{8 \mu_ {2}} \left(R _ {1} + R _ {- 1} - 2 R _ {0}\right) \geq \frac {1}{2} - \frac {\delta}{2} \left(1 + \frac {1 - a}{4} \cdot | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ A _ {i} ^ {- 1} \right] = \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \frac {1}{2} \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x) \right] \\ = \frac {1}{2} R _ {0} + \frac {a}{8 \mu_ {2}} \left(R _ {1} + R _ {- 1} - 2 R _ {0}\right) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \left(R _ {1} - R _ {- 1}\right) \geq \frac {1}{2} - \frac {\delta}{2} \left(1 + \frac {a}{2} | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1} + \frac {1}{4} | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

The independence of the  $A_{i}^{t}$  for each  $t\in \{-1,0,1\}$  implies that

$$
1 - \mathbb {P} \left[ C ^ {t} \right] = \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \left(1 - \mathbb {P} \left[ A _ {i} ^ {t} \right]\right) \leq \left(\frac {1}{2} + \frac {\delta}{2} \left(1 + \frac {1}{2} | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1} + | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1}\right)\right) ^ {N}
$$

Note that  $\mathcal{L}(Z_t|A_i^t)$  are each absolutely continuous with respect to  $\mathcal{Q}$  or each  $t\in \{-1,0,1\}$ , with Radon-Nikodym derivatives given by

$$
\frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {1} | B _ {i} ^ {1})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {1} | A _ {i} ^ {1})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \frac {1}{2 \cdot \mathbb {P} [ A _ {i} ^ {1} ]} \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x)
$$

$$
\frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {0} | B _ {i} ^ {0})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {0} | A _ {i} ^ {0})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \frac {1}{2 \cdot \mathbb {P} [ A _ {i} ^ {1} ]} \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x)
$$

$$
\frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {- 1} | B _ {i} ^ {- 1})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {- 1} | A _ {i} ^ {- 1})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \frac {1}{2 \cdot \mathbb {P} [ A _ {i} ^ {1} ]} \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x)
$$

Fix one of  $t\in \{-1,0,1\}$  and note that since the conditional laws  $\mathcal{L}(Z_t|B_i^t)$  are all identical, we have that

$$
\frac {d \mathcal {D} _ {t}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \mathbb {P} \left[ C ^ {t} \right] \cdot \frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {t} | B _ {1} ^ {t})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) + \left(1 - \mathbb {P} \left[ C ^ {t} \right]\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {z _ {0}} (x)
$$

Therefore it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {D} _ {t}, \mathcal {L} (Z _ {t} | B _ {1} ^ {t})\right) = \frac {1}{2} \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| \frac {d \mathcal {D} _ {t}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {t} | B _ {1} ^ {t})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) \right| \right] \\ \leq \frac {1}{2} \left(1 - \mathbb {P} \left[ C ^ {t} \right]\right) \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \mathbf {1} _ {z _ {0}} (x) + \frac {d \mathcal {L} (Z _ {t} | B _ {1} ^ {t})}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) \right] = 1 - \mathbb {P} \left[ C ^ {t} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

by the triangle inequality. Since  $1 + \frac{a}{4\mu_2} \cdot \mathcal{L}_2(x) + \frac{1}{4\mu_1} \cdot \mathcal{L}_1(x) \geq 0$  for  $x \in S$ , we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| \frac {d \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {1} \mid B _ {1} ^ {1}\right)}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \right| \right] \\ = \left| \frac {1}{2 \cdot \mathbb {P} \left[ A _ {i} ^ {1} \right]} - 1 \right| \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q} _ {n} ^ {*}} \left[ \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S} (x) \right] \\ + \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| 1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 | \mu_ {1} |} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x) \right| \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S ^ {C}} (x) \right] \\ \leq \left| \frac {1}{2} - \mathbb {P} [ A _ {i} ^ {1} ] \right| + \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 | \mu_ {2} |} \cdot \left(\frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {+}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) + \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {-}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) + 2\right)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S ^ {C}} (x) \right] \\ + \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \frac {1}{4 | \mu_ {1} |} \cdot \left(\frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {+}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) + \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {-}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x)\right) \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {S ^ {C}} (x) \right] \\ \leq \frac {\delta}{2} \left(1 + \frac {a}{2} | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1} + \frac {1}{4} | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1}\right) + \delta \left(1 + a | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1} + \frac {1}{2} | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1}\right) = \delta \left(\frac {3}{2} + \frac {5}{4} | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1} + \frac {5}{8} | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

By analogous computations, we have that

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| \frac {d \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {0} \mid B _ {1} ^ {0}\right)}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \right| \right] \leq 2 \delta \left(1 + | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1} + | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1}\right)
$$

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| \frac {d \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {- 1} \mid B _ {1} ^ {- 1}\right)}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \right| \right] \leq 2 \delta \left(1 + | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1} + | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1}\right)
$$

Now observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {+}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} + \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) + (a - 2 \mu_ {2}) \cdot \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \\ + \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} - \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \\ 1 = \frac {1 - a}{2} \cdot \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) + a \cdot \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \\ + \frac {1 - a}{2} \cdot \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {d \mathcal {P} _ {-}}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) = \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} - \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) + (a - 2 \mu_ {2}) \cdot \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \\ + \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} + \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Let  $\mathcal{D}^*$  be the mixture of  $\mathcal{L}(Z_1|B_1^1),\mathcal{L}(Z_0|B_1^0)$  and  $\mathcal{L}(Z_{-1}|B_1^{-1})$  with weights  $\frac{1 - a}{2} +\mu_1 + \mu_2,a - 2\mu_2$  and  $\frac{1 - a}{2} -\mu_1 + \mu_2$  , respectively. It then follows by the triangle inequality that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(3 - \operatorname {S R K} \left(\operatorname {T e r n} \left(a, \mu_ {1}, \mu_ {2}\right)\right), \mathcal {P} _ {+}\right) \\ \leq d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {D} ^ {*}, \mathcal {P} _ {+}\right) + d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {D} ^ {*}, 3 - \operatorname {S R K} (\operatorname {T e r n} (a, \mu_ {1}, \mu_ {2}))\right) \\ \leq \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} + \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| \frac {d \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {1} \mid B _ {1} ^ {1}\right)}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) + \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right) \right| \right] \\ \left. + \left(a - 2 \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[ \left| \frac {d \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {0} \mid B _ {1} ^ {0}\right)}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \left(1 - \frac {1 - a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x)\right) \right| \right] \right. \\ \left. \right. + \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} - \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {Q}} \left[\left| \frac {d \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {- 1} \mid B _ {1} ^ {- 1}\right)}{d \mathcal {Q}} (x) - \left(1 + \frac {a}{4 \mu_ {2}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {2} (x) - \frac {1}{4 \mu_ {1}} \cdot \mathcal {L} _ {1} (x)\right)\right|\right] \\ + \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} + \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {D} _ {1}, \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {1} \mid B _ {1} ^ {1}\right)\right) + \left(a - 2 \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {D} _ {1}, \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {0} \mid B _ {1} ^ {0}\right)\right) \\ + \left(\frac {1 - a}{2} - \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2}\right) \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {D} _ {- 1}, \mathcal {L} \left(Z _ {- 1} \mid B _ {1} ^ {- 1}\right)\right) \\ \leq 2 \delta \left(1 + | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1} + | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1}\right) + \left(\frac {1}{2} + \delta \left(1 + | \mu_ {1} | ^ {- 1} + | \mu_ {2} | ^ {- 1}\right)\right) ^ {N} \\ \end{array}
$$

A symmetric argument shows analogous upper bounds on both  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$  (3-SRK(Tern  $(a, - \mu_1,\mu_2))$ $\mathcal{P}_{-}$  ) and  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$  (3-SRK(Tern(a,0,0)),  $\mathcal{Q}$  ), completing the proof of the lemma.

# A.4 Proofs for Label Generation

In this section, we give the two deferred proofs from Section 10.2.

Proof of Lemma 10.10. This lemma follows from a similar argument to Lemma 10.9. As in Lemma 10.9, the given conditions on  $C, \gamma, \mu'$  and  $N$  imply that

$$
2 \left(\frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})}\right) ^ {2} \leq 1
$$

and thus  $X^{\prime}$  is well-defined almost surely. First observe that if  $Z = \mu^{\prime \prime}\cdot u + G^{\prime}$  where  $G^{\prime}\sim \mathcal{N}(0,I_d)$  then

$$
X ^ {\prime} = \frac {a \gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{1 + \gamma^ {2}} \cdot u + \frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})} \cdot G ^ {\prime} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \cdot \sqrt {1 - 2 \left(\frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})}\right) ^ {2}} \cdot G + \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \cdot W
$$

where  $a = \mu'' / \mu'$ . Thus by the same argument as in Lemma 10.9, we have that

$$
\mathcal {L} \left(X ^ {\prime} \mid y ^ {\prime}\right) = \mathcal {N} \left(\frac {a \gamma \cdot y}{1 + \gamma^ {2}} \cdot u, I _ {d} - \frac {\gamma^ {2}}{1 + \gamma^ {2}} \cdot u u ^ {\top}\right)
$$

Now note that by the conditioning property of multivariate Gaussians, we have that

$$
\mathcal {L} (X | y) = \mathcal {N} \left(\Sigma_ {X y} \Sigma_ {y y} ^ {- 1} \cdot y, \Sigma_ {X X} - \Sigma_ {X y} \Sigma_ {y y} ^ {- 1} \Sigma_ {y X}\right)
$$

It is easily verified that

$$
\Sigma_ {X y} \Sigma_ {y y} ^ {- 1} = \frac {a \gamma}{1 + \gamma^ {2}} \cdot u \quad \text {a n d} \quad \Sigma_ {X X} - \Sigma_ {X y} \Sigma_ {y y} ^ {- 1} \Sigma_ {y X} = I _ {d} - \frac {\gamma^ {2}}{1 + \gamma^ {2}} \cdot u u ^ {\top}
$$

and thus  $\mathcal{L}(X|y)$  and  $\mathcal{L}(X'|y')$  are equidistributed. Since  $y \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1 + \gamma^2)$ , it follows by the same application of the conditioning property in Fact 6.2 as in Lemma 10.9 implies that

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} (X, y), \mathcal {L} \left(X ^ {\prime}, y ^ {\prime}\right)\right) \leq d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {L} (y), \mathcal {L} \left(y ^ {\prime}\right)\right) = O \left(N ^ {- C ^ {2} / 2}\right)
$$

which completes the proof of the lemma.

Proof of Lemma 10.11. This lemma follows from a similar argument to Lemma 10.9. As in Lemmas 10.9 and 10.10, the given conditions imply that  $X'$  is well-defined almost surely. Conditioned on  $y'$ , it holds that  $Z, G$  and  $W$  are independent. Therefore the three terms in the definition of  $X'$  are independent and distributed as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})} \cdot Z \sim \mathcal {N} \left(0, \left(\frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})}\right) ^ {2} \cdot I _ {d}\right), \\ \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \cdot \sqrt {1 - 2 \left(\frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})}\right) ^ {2}} \cdot G \sim \mathcal {N} \left(0, \frac {1}{2} \cdot I _ {d} - \left(\frac {\gamma \cdot y ^ {\prime}}{\mu^ {\prime} (1 + \gamma^ {2})}\right) ^ {2} \cdot I _ {d}\right) \quad \text {a n d} \\ \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} \cdot W \sim \mathcal {N} \left(0, \frac {1}{2} \cdot I _ {d}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

conditioned on  $y^\prime$ . It follows that  $X^{\prime}|y^{\prime}\sim \mathcal{N}(0,I_d)$  and thus  $X^{\prime}$  is independent of  $y^\prime$ . Now let  $X\in \mathbb{R}^{d}$  and  $y\in \mathbb{R}$  be such that  $X\sim \mathcal{N}(0,I_d)$  and  $y\sim \mathcal{N}(0,1 + \gamma^2)$  are independent. The same application of the conditioning property in Fact 6.2 as in Lemmas 10.9 and 10.10 now completes the proof of the lemma.

# B Deferred Proofs from Part III

# B.1 Proofs from Secret Leakage and the  $\mathrm{PC}_{\rho}$  Conjecture

In this section, we present the deferred proof of Lemma 12.15 from Section 12. The proof of this lemma is similar to the proof of Lemma 5.2 in  $\left[\mathrm{FGR}^{+}13\right]$ .

Proof of Lemma 12.15. The proof is almost identical to Lemma 5.2 in  $\left[\mathrm{FGR}^{+}13\right]$  and we give a sketch here. Lemma 12.14 implies that  $\sum_{T\in A}\left|\langle \widehat{D}_S,\widehat{D}_T\rangle_D\right|\leq \sum_{T\in A}2^{|S\cap T|}k^2 /n^2$ . If the only constraint on  $A$  is its cardinality, then the maximum value for the RHS is obtained by adding  $S$  to  $A$ , next  $\{T:|T\cap S| = k - 1\}$ , and so forth with decreasing size of  $|T\cap S|$ , and we assume that  $A$  is defined in this manner. Letting  $T_{\lambda} = \{T:|T\cap S| = \lambda \}$ , set  $\lambda_0 = \min \{\lambda :T_\lambda \neq \emptyset \}$  so that  $T_{\lambda}\subseteq A$  for  $\lambda >\lambda_0$ . We bound the ratio

$$
\frac {| T _ {j} |}{| T _ {j + 1} |} = \frac {\binom {k} {j} \left(\frac {n}{k}\right) ^ {k - j}}{\binom {k} {j + 1} \left(\frac {n}{k}\right) ^ {k - j - 1}} \geq \frac {j n}{k ^ {2}} = j n ^ {2 \delta} \quad \text {h e n c e} \quad | T _ {j} | \leq \frac {| T _ {0} |}{(j - 1) ! n ^ {2 \delta j}} \leq \frac {| S |}{(j - 1) ! n ^ {2 \delta j}}.
$$

Now

$$
| A | \leq \sum_ {j \geq \lambda_ {0}} | T _ {j} | \leq | \mathcal {S} | n ^ {- 2 \delta \lambda_ {0}} \sum_ {j \geq \lambda_ {0}} \frac {1}{(j - 1) ! n ^ {2 \delta (j - \lambda_ {0})}} \leq 2 | \mathcal {S} | n ^ {- 2 \delta \lambda_ {0}}
$$

for  $n$  greater than some constant. Thus if  $|A| \geq 2|S| / n^{2\ell \delta}$ , we must conclude that  $\ell \geq \lambda_0$ . We bound the quantity  $\sum_{T \in A} 2^{|S \cap T|} \leq \sum_{j = \lambda_0}^{k} 2^j |T_j \cap A| \leq 2^{\lambda_0} |T_{\lambda_0} \cap A| + \sum_{j = \lambda_0 + 1}^{k} 2^j |T_j| \leq 2^{\lambda_0} |A| + 2^{\lambda_0 + 2} |T_{\lambda_0 + 1}| \leq 2^{\lambda_0 + 3} |A| \leq 2^{\ell + 3} |A|$ . Here we used that  $|T_{j + 1}| \leq |T_j| n^{-2\delta}$  to bound by a geometric series and also that  $T_{\lambda_0 + 1} \subseteq A$ . Rearranging and combining with the inequality at the start of the proof concludes the argument.

# B.2 Proofs for Reductions and Computational Lower Bounds

In this section, we present a number of deferred proofs from Part III. The majority of these proofs are similar to other proofs presented in the main body of the paper.

Proof of Theorem 3.7. To prove this theorem, we will to show that Theorem 10.8 implies that  $k$ -BPDS-TO-MSLR applied with  $r > 2$  fills out all of the possible growth rates specified by the computational lower bound  $n = \tilde{o}(k^2\epsilon^2/\tau^4)$  and the other conditions in the theorem statement. As discussed above, it suffices to reduce in total variation to  $\mathrm{MSLR}(n,k,d,\tau,1/r)$  where  $1/r \leq \epsilon$ .

Fix a constant pair of probabilities  $0 < q < p \leq 1$  and any sequence of parameters  $(n, k, d, \tau, \epsilon)$  all of which are implicitly functions of  $n$  such that  $(n, \epsilon^{-1})$  satisfies (T) and  $(n, k, d, \tau, \epsilon)$  satisfy the conditions

$$
n \leq c \cdot \frac {k ^ {2} \epsilon^ {2}}{w ^ {2} \cdot \tau^ {4} \cdot (\log n) ^ {4 + 2 c ^ {\prime}}}, \quad w k \leq n ^ {1 / 6} \quad \text {a n d} \quad w k ^ {2} \leq d
$$

for sufficiently large  $n$ , an arbitrarily slow-growing function  $w = w(n) \to \infty$  at least satisfying that  $w(n) = n^{o(1)}$ , a sufficiently small constant  $c > 0$  and a sufficiently large constant  $c' > 0$ . The rest of this proof will follow that of Theorem 3.1 very closely. In order to fulfill the criteria in Condition 6.1, we specify  $M, N, k_M, k_N$  and  $n'$  exactly as in Theorem 3.1. As in Theorem 3.1, we have the inequalities

$$
n ^ {\prime} \leq w ^ {- 2} r ^ {2 t} = O \left(\frac {r ^ {2 t}}{n} \cdot \frac {k ^ {2} \epsilon^ {2}}{\tau^ {4} \cdot (\log n) ^ {2 + 2 c ^ {\prime}}}\right)
$$

$$
\tau \leq \frac {c ^ {1 / 4} \epsilon^ {1 / 2} k ^ {1 / 2}}{n ^ {1 / 4} (\log n) ^ {(2 + c ^ {\prime}) / 2}} = \Theta \left(\frac {r ^ {t / 2}}{n ^ {1 / 4}} \cdot \frac {k _ {M} ^ {1 / 2}}{\sqrt {r ^ {t + 1} (\log n) ^ {2 + c ^ {\prime}}}}\right)
$$

Furthermore, we also have that

$$
\tau^ {2} \leq \frac {c ^ {1 / 2} \cdot k}{w n ^ {1 / 2} \cdot (\log n) ^ {2 + c ^ {\prime}}} = O \left(\frac {r ^ {t}}{n} \cdot \frac {k _ {N} k _ {M}}{N \log (M N)}\right)
$$

As long as  $\sqrt{n} = \tilde{\Theta}(r^t)$  then: (2.1) the inequality above on  $n'$  would imply that  $(n', k, d, \tau, \epsilon)$  is in the desired hard regime; (2.2)  $n$  and  $n'$  have the same growth rate since  $w = n^{o(1)}$ ; and (2.3)  $n \gg M^3$ ,  $d \geq M$  and taking  $c'$  large enough would imply that  $\tau$  satisfies the bounds needed to apply Theorem 10.8 to yield the desired reduction. By Lemma 13.2, there is an infinite subsequence of the input parameters such that  $\sqrt{n} = \tilde{\Theta}(r^t)$ , which concludes the proof as in Theorem 3.1.

Proof of Lemma 14.8. First suppose that  $M \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_D(n, r, C, D, \gamma)$  where  $C$  and  $D$  are each sequences of  $r$  disjoint sets of size  $K$ . Since the  $M_{ij}$  are independent for  $1 \leq i, j \leq n$ , we now have that

$$
\mathbb {E} [ s _ {C} (M) ] = \sum_ {i, j = 1} ^ {n} \mathbb {E} \left[ M _ {i j} ^ {2} - 1 \right] = r K ^ {2} \cdot \gamma^ {2} + \frac {r K ^ {2}}{r - 1} \cdot \gamma^ {2}
$$

$$
\operatorname {V a r} \left[ s _ {C} (M) \right] = \sum_ {i, j = 1} ^ {n} \operatorname {V a r} \left[ M _ {i j} ^ {2} - 1 \right] = r K ^ {2} \cdot 4 \gamma^ {2} + \frac {r K ^ {2}}{(r - 1) ^ {3}} \cdot \gamma^ {2} + 2 n ^ {2}
$$

Here, we have used the following facts. If  $X \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$ , then

$$
\mathbb {E} [ (\gamma + X) ^ {2} - 1 ] = \gamma^ {2}, \quad \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(\frac {\gamma}{r - 1} + X\right) ^ {2} - 1 \right] = \frac {\gamma^ {2}}{(r - 1) ^ {2}}
$$

$$
\operatorname {V a r} [ X ^ {2} - 1 ] = 2, \quad \operatorname {V a r} [ (\gamma + X) ^ {2} - 1 ] = 4 \gamma^ {2} + 2, \quad \operatorname {V a r} \left[ \left(\frac {\gamma}{r - 1} + X\right) ^ {2} - 1 \right] = \frac {\gamma^ {2}}{(r - 1) ^ {4}} + 2
$$

Note that  $s_C(M)$  is invariant to permuting the rows and columns of  $M$  and thus  $s_C(M)$  is equidistributed under  $M \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_D(n, r, C, D, \gamma)$  and  $M \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_D(n, r, K, \gamma)$ . Now Chebyshev's inequality implies the desired lower bound on  $s_C(M)$  in (1) holds with probability  $1 - o_n(1)$ . Now observe that

$$
s _ {I} (M) \geq \sum_ {h = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in C _ {h}} \sum_ {j \in D _ {h}} M _ {i j} = Y
$$

holds almost surely by definition when  $M \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_D(n, r, C, D, \gamma)$ . Note that  $Y \sim \mathcal{N}(rK^2\gamma, rK^2)$  conditioned on  $C$  and  $D$  and therefore it holds that  $Y \geq rK^2\gamma - wr^{1/2}K$  with probability  $1 - o_n(1)$ . The second lower bound in (1) now follows since  $s_I(M)$  is equidistributed under  $M \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_D(n, r, C, D, \gamma)$  and  $M \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_D(n, r, K, \gamma)$ .

Now suppose that  $M \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n \times n}$ . In this case,  $s_C(M) + n^2$  is distributed as  $\chi^2(n^2)$  and the first upper bound in (2) holds by Chebyshev's inequality and the fact that  $\chi^2(n^2)$  has variance  $2n^2$ . Now note

$$
Y (C, D) = \sum_ {h = 1} ^ {r} \sum_ {i \in C _ {h}} \sum_ {j \in D _ {h}} M _ {i j} \sim \mathcal {N} (0, r K ^ {2})
$$

Standard gaussian tail bounds imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ Y (C, D) > 2 r K ^ {3 / 2} w \sqrt {(\log n + \log r)} \right] \leq \frac {1}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \exp \left(- \frac {1}{2 r K ^ {2}} \left(2 r K ^ {3 / 2} w \sqrt {(\log n + \log r)}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq (n r) ^ {- 2 r K w ^ {2}} \\ \end{array}
$$

A crude upper bound on the number of pairs  $(C, D)$  is

$$
\left(\binom {n} {r K} r ^ {r K}\right) ^ {2} = o \left((n r) ^ {2 r K}\right)
$$

and therefore a union bound implies that  $s_I(M) = \max_{C,D} Y(C, D) \leq 2rK^{3/2} w\sqrt{(\log n + \log r)}$  with probability  $1 - o_n(1)$ . This completes the proof of the lemma.

Proof of Corollary 14.11. Consider the following reduction  $\mathcal{A}$  that adds a simple post-processing step to  $k$ -PDS-TO-GHPM as in Corollary 14.5. On input graph  $G$  with  $N$  vertices:

1. Form the graph  $M_{\mathrm{R}}$  by applying  $k$ -PDS-TO-GHPM to  $G$  with parameters  $N, r, k, E, \ell, n, s$  and  $\mu$  where  $\mu$  is given by

$$
\mu = \frac {r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}{(r - 1)} \cdot \Phi^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{2} + \frac {1}{2} \cdot \min  \{P _ {0}, 1 - P _ {0} \} ^ {- 1} \cdot \gamma\right)
$$

and  $\Phi^{-1}$  is the inverse of the standard normal CDF.

2. Let  $G_{1}$  be the graph where each edge  $(i,j)$  with is in  $G_{1}$  if and only if  $(M_{\mathbb{R}})_{ij} \geq 0$ . Now form  $G_{2}$  as in Step 2 of Corollary 14.5, while restricting to edges between the two parts.

This clearly runs in  $\mathrm{poly}(N)$  time and it suffices to establish its approximate Markov transition properties. Let  $\mathcal{A}_1$  denote the first step with input  $G$  and output  $M_{\mathrm{R}}$ , and let  $\mathcal{A}_2$  denote the second step with input  $M_{\mathrm{R}}$  and output  $G_2$ . Let  $C$  and  $D$  be two fixed sequences, each consisting of  $r$  disjoint subsets of  $[ksr^t]$  of size  $kr^{t-1}$ . Let  $P_1, P_2 \in (0,1)$  be

$$
P _ {1} = \Phi \left(\frac {\mu (r - 1)}{r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}\right) \quad \text {a n d} \quad P _ {2} = \Phi \left(- \frac {\mu}{r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}\right)
$$

Note that by the definition of  $\mu$ , we have that  $P_{1} = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} \cdot \min \{P_{0}, 1 - P_{0}\}^{-1} \cdot \gamma$ . Now note that  $\mathcal{A}_{2}$  applied to  $M_{\mathrm{R}} \sim \mathrm{GHPM}_{D}(ksr^{t}, r, C, D, \gamma)$  yields an instance of  $\mathrm{BHPM}_{D}(ksr^{t}, r, C, D, \gamma)$  with the following modified edge probabilities:

1. The edge probabilities between vertices  $C_h$  and  $D_h$  for each  $1 \leq h \leq r$  are still  $P_0 + \gamma$ .  
2. The edge probabilities between  $C_{h_1}$  and  $D_{h_2}$  for each  $h_1 \neq h_2$  are now

$$
P _ {0} + 2 \min  \left\{P _ {0}, 1 - P _ {0} \right\} \cdot \left(\Phi \left(- \frac {\mu}{r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}\right) - \frac {1}{2}\right) = P _ {0} + 2 \min  \left\{P _ {0}, 1 - P _ {0} \right\} \cdot \left(P _ {2} - \frac {1}{2}\right)
$$

3. All other edge probabilities are still  $P_0$ .

We now apply a similar sequence of inequalities as in Corollary 14.5. For now assume that  $P_0 \leq 1/2$ . Using the fact that all of the edge indicators of this model and the usual definition of BHPM are independent, the tensorization property in Fact 6.2 and Lemma 6.5, we now have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {A} _ {2} \left(\mathrm {G H P M} _ {D} (k s r ^ {t}, r, C, D, \gamma)\right), \mathrm {B H P M} _ {D} (k s r ^ {t}, r, C, D, \gamma)\right) \\ \leq d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\operatorname {B e r n} \left(P _ {0} - \frac {\gamma}{r - 1}\right) ^ {\otimes k ^ {2} r ^ {2 t - 1} (r - 1)}, \operatorname {B e r n} \left(P _ {0} + 2 P _ {0} \cdot \left(P _ {2} - \frac {1}{2}\right)\right) ^ {\otimes k ^ {2} r ^ {2 t - 1} (r - 1)}\right) \\ \leq \left| \frac {\gamma}{r - 1} + 2 P _ {0} \cdot \left(P _ {2} - \frac {1}{2}\right) \right| \cdot \sqrt {\frac {k ^ {2} r ^ {2 t - 1} (r - 1)}{2 \left(P _ {0} - \frac {\gamma}{r - 1}\right) \left(1 - P _ {0} + \frac {\gamma}{r - 1}\right)}} \\ \leq \left| \frac {\gamma}{r - 1} + 2 P _ {0} \cdot \left(P _ {2} - \frac {1}{2}\right) \right| \cdot O (k r ^ {t}) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third inequality uses the fact that  $P_0$  is bounded away from 0 and 1 and  $\gamma = o(1)$ . Now note that

$$
\frac {\gamma}{r - 1} = \frac {2 P _ {0}}{r - 1} \cdot \left(\Phi \left(\frac {\mu (r - 1)}{r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}\right) - \frac {1}{2}\right)
$$

Using the standard Taylor approximation for  $\Phi (x) - 1 / 2$  around zero when  $x\in (-1,1)$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {\gamma}{r - 1} + 2 P _ {0} \cdot \left(P _ {2} - \frac {1}{2}\right) \right| = 2 P _ {0} \cdot \left| \frac {1}{r - 1} \left(\Phi \left(\frac {\mu (r - 1)}{r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}\right) - \frac {1}{2}\right) - \left(\Phi \left(- \frac {\mu}{r ^ {t} \sqrt {r}}\right) - \frac {1}{2}\right) \right| \\ = O \left(\frac {\mu^ {3} \sqrt {r}}{r ^ {3 t}}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore we have that

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(\mathcal {A} _ {2} \left(\mathrm {G H P M} _ {D} (k s r ^ {t}, r, C, D, \gamma)\right), \mathrm {B H P M} _ {D} (k s r ^ {t}, r, C, D, \gamma)\right) = O \left(\frac {k \mu^ {3} \sqrt {r}}{r ^ {2 t}}\right)
$$

A nearly identical argument considering the complement of the graph  $G_{1}$  and replacing with  $P_{0}$  with  $1 - P_{0}$  establishes this bound in the case when  $P_{0} > 1 / 2$ . Observe that  $\mathcal{A}_2(\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times n})\sim \mathcal{G}_B(n,n,P_0)$ . Now consider applying Lemma 6.3 to the steps  $\mathcal{A}_1$  and  $\mathcal{A}_2$  as in Corollary 14.5. It can be verified that the given bound on  $\gamma$  yields the condition on  $\mu$  needed to apply Theorem 14.9 if  $c > 0$  is sufficiently small. Thus  $\epsilon_{1}$  is bounded by Theorem 14.9 and  $\epsilon_{2}$  is bounded by the argument above after averaging over  $C$  and  $D$  and applying the conditioning property of Fact 6.2. This application of Lemma 6.3 therefore yields the desired two approximate Markov transition properties and completes the proof of the corollary.

Proof of Theorem 3.4. As discussed in the beginning of this section, it suffices to map to  $\mathcal{G}(n,P_0 - \mu_1)$  under  $H_{0}$  and  $\mathrm{TSI}(n,k,k_1,P_0,\mu_1,\mu_2,\mu_3)$  under  $H_{1}$  where  $\mu_{3} = P_{1} - P_{0}$  and  $\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\geq 0$ . Thus it suffices to show that the reduction  $\mathcal{A}$  in Corollary 14.15 fills out all of the possible growth rates specified by the computational lower bound  $\frac{(P_1 - P_0)^2}{P_0(1 - P_0)} = \tilde{o} (n / k^2)$  and the other conditions in the theorem statement. Fix a constant pair of probabilities  $0 < q < p\leq 1$  and any sequence of parameters  $(n,k,P_1,P_0)$  all of which are implicitly functions of  $n$  such that

$$
\frac {(P _ {1} - P _ {0}) ^ {2}}{P _ {0} (1 - P _ {0})} \leq c \cdot \frac {n}{w ^ {3} \cdot k ^ {2} \log n} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \min  \{P _ {0}, 1 - P _ {0} \} = \Omega_ {n} (1)
$$

for sufficiently large  $n$ , sufficiently small constant  $c > 0$  and an arbitrarily slow-growing increasing positive integer-valued function  $w = w(n) \to \infty$  at least satisfying that  $w(n) = n^{o(1)}$ . As in the proof of Theorem 3.1, it suffices to specify:

1. a sequence  $(N,k_N)$  such that the  $k$ -PDS  $(N,k_N,p,q)$  is hard according to Conjecture 2.3; and  
2. a sequence  $(n', k', P_1, P_0, s, t, \mu)$  satisfying: (2.1) the parameters  $(n', k', P_1, P_0)$  are in the regime of the desired computational lower bound for SEMI-CR; (2.2)  $(n', k')$  have the same growth rates as  $(n, k)$ ; and (2.3) such that  $\mathcal{G}(n', P_0 - \mu_1)$  and  $\mathrm{TSI}(n', k', k'/2, P_0, \mu_1, \mu_2, P_1 - P_0)$ , where  $k'$  is even and  $\mu_1, \mu_2 \geq 0$ , can be produced by  $\mathcal{A}$  with input  $k$ -PDS  $(N, k_N, p, q)$ .

We choose these parameters as follows:

- let  $t$  be such that  $3^t$  is the smallest power of 3 larger than  $k / \sqrt{n}$  and let  $s = \lceil 2n / 3k \rceil$ ;  
- let  $\mu \in (0,1)$  be given by

$$
\mu = 3 ^ {t} \cdot \Phi^ {- 1} \left(\frac {1}{2} + \frac {1}{2} \cdot \min \{P _ {0}, 1 - P _ {0} \} ^ {- 1} (P _ {1} - P _ {0})\right)
$$

- now let

$$
k _ {N} = \left\lfloor \frac {1}{2} \left(1 + \frac {p}{Q}\right) ^ {- 1} w ^ {- 2} \cdot \sqrt {n} \right\rfloor
$$

where  $Q = 1 - \sqrt{(1 - p)(1 - q)} +\mathbf{1}_{\{p = 1\}}(\sqrt{q} -1)$ ; and

- let  $n' = 3k_N s \cdot \frac{3^t - 1}{2}$ , let  $k' = (3^t - 1)k_N$  and let  $N = wk_N^2$ .

Note that  $3^t = \Theta (k / \sqrt{n})$ ,  $s = \Theta (n / k)$  and  $3^{t}k_{N}s\leq \mathrm{poly}(N)$ . Note that this choice of  $\mu$  implies that

$$
P _ {1} = P _ {0} + 2 \min  \{P _ {0}, 1 - P _ {0} \} \cdot \left(\Phi \left(\frac {\mu}{3 ^ {t}}\right) - \frac {1}{2}\right)
$$

which implies that the instance of TSI output by  $\mathcal{A}$  has edge density  $P_{1}$  on its  $k^{\prime}$ -vertex the planted dense subgraph. It follows that

$$
n ^ {\prime} \asymp 3 ^ {t} k _ {N} s \asymp \frac {k}{\sqrt {n}} \cdot \frac {n}{k} w ^ {- 2} \cdot \sqrt {n} \asymp w ^ {- 2} \cdot n \quad \text {a n d} \quad k ^ {\prime} \asymp 3 ^ {t} k _ {N} \asymp w ^ {- 2} k
$$

$$
\frac {(P _ {1} - P _ {0}) ^ {2}}{P _ {0} (1 - P _ {0})} \leq c \cdot \frac {n}{w ^ {3} \cdot k ^ {2} \log n} \lesssim c \cdot \frac {n ^ {\prime}}{w \cdot (k ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} \log n ^ {\prime}}
$$

$$
m \leq 2 \left(\frac {p}{Q} + 1\right) w k _ {N} ^ {2} \leq w ^ {- 1} \sqrt {n} \cdot k _ {N} \leq 3 ^ {t} k _ {N} s
$$

$$
\mu \lesssim 3 ^ {t} \cdot (P _ {1} - P _ {0}) \lesssim 3 ^ {t} \cdot \frac {\sqrt {n}}{w ^ {3 / 2} \cdot k \sqrt {\log n ^ {\prime}}} \leq \frac {c}{w ^ {3 / 2} \sqrt {\log n ^ {\prime}}}
$$

where the last bound above follows from the fact that  $\Phi(x) - 1/2 \sim x$  if  $|x| \to 0$ . Here,  $m$  is the smallest multiple of  $k_N$  larger  $\left(\frac{p}{Q} + 1\right)N$ . Now note that: (2.1) the third inequality above on  $(P_1 - P_0)^2 / P_0(1 - P_0)$  implies that  $(n', k', P_1, P_0)$  is in the desired hard regime; (2.2)  $(n, n')$  and  $(k, k')$  have the same growth rates since  $w = n^{o(1)}$ ; and (2.3) the last two bounds above imply that taking  $c$  small enough yields the conditions needed to apply Corollary 14.15 to yield the desired reduction. This completes the proof of the theorem.

Proof of Lemma 16.1. The parameters  $a, \mu_1, \mu_2$  for which these distributional statements are true are given by

$$
a = \Phi (\tau) - \Phi (- \tau)
$$

$$
\mu_ {1} = \frac {1}{2} \left(\left(1 - \Phi (\tau - \mu)\right) - \Phi (- \tau - \mu)\right) = \frac {1}{2} \left(\Phi (\tau + \mu) - \Phi (\tau - \mu)\right)
$$

$$
\mu_ {2} = \frac {1}{2} (\Phi (\tau) - \Phi (- \tau)) - \frac {1}{2} (\Phi (\tau + \mu) - \Phi (- \tau + \mu)) = \frac {1}{2} (2 \cdot \Phi (\tau) - \Phi (\tau + \mu) - \Phi (\tau - \mu))
$$

Now note that

$$
\mu_ {1} = \frac {1}{2} \left(\Phi (\tau + \mu) - \Phi (\tau - \mu)\right) = \frac {1}{2 \sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {\tau - \mu} ^ {\tau + \mu} e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2} d t = \Theta (\mu)
$$

and is positive since  $e^{-t^2 / 2}$  is bounded on  $[\tau - \mu, \tau + \mu]$  as  $\tau$  is constant and  $\mu \to 0$ . Furthermore, note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mu_ {2} = \frac {1}{2} \left(2 \cdot \Phi (\tau) - \Phi (\tau + \mu) - \Phi (\tau - \mu)\right) = \frac {1}{2 \sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {\tau - \mu} ^ {\tau} e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2} d t - \frac {1}{2 \sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {\tau} ^ {\tau + \mu} e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2} d t \\ = \frac {1}{2 \sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {\tau} ^ {\tau + \mu} \left(e ^ {- (t - \mu) ^ {2} / 2} - e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2}\right) d t = \frac {1}{2 \sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {\tau} ^ {\tau + \mu} e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2} \left(e ^ {t \mu - \mu^ {2} / 2} - 1\right) d t \\ \end{array}
$$

Now note that as  $\mu \to 0$  and for  $t\in [\tau ,\tau +\mu ]$ , it follows that  $0 < e^{t\mu -\mu^2 /2} - 1 = \Theta (\mu)$ . This implies that  $0 < \mu_{2} = \Theta (\mu^{2})$ , as claimed.

Proof of Theorem 3.10. To prove this theorem, we will to show Theorem 16.2 implies that  $k$ -BPDS-TO-GLSM fills out all of the possible growth rates specified by the computational lower bound  $n = \tilde{o}\left(\tau_{\mathcal{U}}^{-4}\right)$  and the other conditions in the theorem statement, as in the proof of Theorems 3.1 and 13.4. Fix a constant pair of probabilities  $0 < q < p \leq 1$  and any sequence  $(n,k,d,\mathcal{U})$  where  $\mathcal{U} = (\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q},\{\mathcal{P}_{\nu}\}_{\nu \in \mathbb{R}}) \in \mathrm{UC}(n)$  all of which are implicitly functions of  $n$  with

$$
n \leq \frac {c}{\tau_ {\mathcal {U}} ^ {4} \cdot w ^ {2} \cdot (\log n) ^ {2}} \quad \text {a n d} \quad w k ^ {2} \leq d
$$

for sufficiently large  $n$ , an arbitrarily slow-growing function  $w = w(n) \to \infty$  and a sufficiently small constant  $c > 0$ . Now consider specifying the parameters  $M, N, k_M, k_N$  and  $t$  exactly as in Theorem 13.4. Now note that under these parameter settings, we have that

$$
\tau_ {\mathcal {U}} \leq \frac {c ^ {1 / 4}}{n ^ {1 / 4} w ^ {1 / 2} \sqrt {\log n}} \leq 2 c ^ {1 / 4} \cdot \sqrt {\frac {k _ {N}}{N \log N}}
$$

Therefore  $\tau_{\mathcal{U}}$  satisfies the conditions needed to apply Theorem 16.2 for a sufficiently small  $c > 0$ . The other parameters  $(n, k, d, \mathcal{U})$  and  $(M, N, k_M, k_N, p, q)$  can also be verified to satisfy the conditions of this theorem. We now have that  $k$ -BPDS $(M, N, k_M, k_N, p, q)$  is hard according to Conjecture 2.3, and that GLSM $(n, k, d, \mathcal{U})$  can be produced by the reduction  $k$ -BPDS-TO-GLSM applied to BPDS $(M, N, k_M, k_N, p, q)$ . This verifies the criteria in Condition 6.1 and, following the argument in Section 6.2, Lemma 6.1 now implies the theorem.
